People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m here because… it’s the voices, mainly. I know how that sounds, but I keep hearing them at work, like coming through the call-light speaker or the hallway vents. It’s not muffled like normal building noise—it’s clear, like somebody is standing right next to me and telling me which patient records have been changed.

And it happens even when the unit is quiet. Like when there’s no one out in the hall, no alarms going off, nobody talking at the desk. I’ll hear it, and I’ll look around, and nobody else seems to hear anything. It’s been really upsetting, because it feels so real in the moment, and I can’t just ignore it when it’s about patient records.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… yes, I think there’s something real happening. I don’t know exactly who is doing it or how, and I know that’s the part that sounds strange, but I can’t just accept that it’s nothing.

The wound-care charts have a pattern. I’ve been looking at them closely because that’s part of what they had me doing, and there are changes that don’t make sense—entries shifted, things coded a certain way, times that don’t line up. My supervisor keeps saying it’s just late entries or coding mistakes, like people are busy and they fix things later, but it doesn’t feel random to me. It’s too consistent.

And then when I write something down or flag it, I get this sense that management is watching what I’m putting in the chart, like they know I’m the one who noticed it. I’m not trying to cause trouble, I’m really not, but I caught something they don’t want people to see. So when I hear the messages through the speakers or the vents, it feels connected to that—like it’s about the records and about me noticing the pattern.

Yes… yes, that’s been happening. I don’t know if I would have called it “energized” at first, because I kept telling myself I was just trying to stay on top of everything, but it’s more than normal tired-but-busy.

I’ll work a full shift, be on my feet all day, and then I get home and instead of crashing like I used to, I’ll start cleaning the kitchen, wiping down counters, reorganizing things, rewriting my notes from work so they’re clearer, sorting supplies or papers into piles. My husband will be sitting there saying, “Please just sit down for a minute,” and I’ll tell him I can’t yet because if I stop, I feel like I’ll lose track of something important.

Yes… I guess I have. I don’t mean like, “special abilities” like a superhero or something. It’s not that. It’s more like… I feel like I’m the one who can see what’s actually happening in the wound-care charts when everyone else is brushing it off.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I mean, not happy exactly, like not carefree happy. It’s more like some days I feel suddenly clear, like I’m finally awake and everybody else is moving too slowly. Like I can see what needs to be fixed at work, and I’m supposed to be the one to fix it or at least point it out.

And when I’m in that state, I can get really impatient. If my husband tells me to slow down or sit down, I snap at him. I don’t mean to, but it comes out sharp, like he’s trying to stop me from doing something important. And if a nurse at work tells me I’m overthinking it or says, “Just leave it alone for now,” I get irritated fast, because it feels like they’re blocking me or dismissing something that matters.

So yes, there are days where I feel… almost charged up, like I have a purpose. But it can turn into anger really quickly if someone gets in my way.

I’m not trying to cause trouble, I’m really not, but I caught something they don’t want people to see. So when I hear the messages through the speakers or the vents, it feels connected to that—like it’s about the records and about me noticing the pattern.\n\nDoctor: During these past two months, have you had a period where you felt unusually energized and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s been happening. I don’t know if I would have called it “energized” at first, because I kept telling myself I was just trying to stay on top of everything, but it’s more than normal tired-but-busy.\n\nI’ll work a full shift, be on my feet all day, and then I get home and instead of crashing like I used to, I’ll start cleaning the kitchen, wiping down counters, reorganizing things, rewriting my notes from work so they’re clearer, sorting supplies or papers into piles. My husband will be sitting there saying, “Please just sit down for a minute,” and I’ll tell him I can’t yet because if I stop, I feel like I’ll lose track of something important.\n\nSometimes I just pace around the kitchen while I’m thinking, back and forth, back and forth, and he gets frustrated because it’s late and he wants me to come to bed. But I don’t feel ready to sleep. My body feels like it still has a motor running, even after the whole day.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you felt like you had special abilities or an unusually important role?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess I have. I don’t mean like, “special abilities” like a superhero or something. It’s not that. It’s more like… I feel like I’m the one who can see what’s actually happening in the wound-care charts when everyone else is brushing it off.\n\nBecause the patterns are there. I know they are. The entries, the times, the way things are coded—it’s not just random mistakes. My supervisor keeps saying, “Oh, those are late entries,” or “That’s just someone fixing documentation,” but I’ve worked with these records enough to know when something doesn’t fit. And it keeps lining up in a way that feels deliberate.\n\nSo yes, I do feel like I have an important role in it, because I caught it. I noticed what management doesn’t want anyone to see. And now when I write things down or flag something, I can tell they’re watching what I put in. It feels like they know I’m the one who saw the pattern, and that makes it hard to just pretend it’s nothing.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you felt unusually happy, euphoric, or especially irritable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not happy exactly, like not carefree happy. It’s more like some days I feel suddenly clear, like I’m finally awake and everybody else is moving too slowly. Like I can see what needs to be fixed at work, and I’m supposed to be the one to fix it or at least point it out.\n\nAnd when I’m in that state, I can get really impatient. If my husband tells me to slow down or sit down, I snap at him. I don’t mean to, but it comes out sharp, like he’s trying to stop me from doing something important. No… I don’t think so. Not when I’m really back to myself.

I don’t know if I would have called it “energized” at first, because I kept telling myself I was just trying to stay on top of everything, but it’s more than normal tired-but-busy.\n\nI’ll work a full shift, be on my feet all day, and then I get home and instead of crashing like I used to, I’ll start cleaning the kitchen, wiping down counters, reorganizing things, rewriting my notes from work so they’re clearer, sorting supplies or papers into piles. My husband will be sitting there saying, “Please just sit down for a minute,” and I’ll tell him I can’t yet because if I stop, I feel like I’ll lose track of something important.\n\nSometimes I just pace around the kitchen while I’m thinking, back and forth, back and forth, and he gets frustrated because it’s late and he wants me to come to bed. But I don’t feel ready to sleep. My body feels like it still has a motor running, even after the whole day.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you felt like you had special abilities or an unusually important role?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess I have. I don’t mean like, “special abilities” like a superhero or something. It’s not that. It’s more like… I feel like I’m the one who can see what’s actually happening in the wound-care charts when everyone else is brushing it off.\n\nBecause the patterns are there. I know they are. The entries, the times, the way things are coded—it’s not just random mistakes. My supervisor keeps saying, “Oh, those are late entries,” or “That’s just someone fixing documentation,” but I’ve worked with these records enough to know when something doesn’t fit. And it keeps lining up in a way that feels deliberate.\n\nSo yes, I do feel like I have an important role in it, because I caught it. I noticed what management doesn’t want anyone to see. And now when I write things down or flag something, I can tell they’re watching what I put in. It feels like they know I’m the one who saw the pattern, and that makes it hard to just pretend it’s nothing.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you felt unusually happy, euphoric, or especially irritable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not happy exactly, like not carefree happy. It’s more like some days I feel suddenly clear, like I’m finally awake and everybody else is moving too slowly. Like I can see what needs to be fixed at work, and I’m supposed to be the one to fix it or at least point it out.\n\nAnd when I’m in that state, I can get really impatient. If my husband tells me to slow down or sit down, I snap at him. I don’t mean to, but it comes out sharp, like he’s trying to stop me from doing something important. And if a nurse at work tells me I’m overthinking it or says, “Just leave it alone for now,” I get irritated fast, because it feels like they’re blocking me or dismissing something that matters.\n\nSo yes, there are days where I feel… almost charged up, like I have a purpose. Yes. Definitely more than seven days. It’s been… I guess about two months now, since they asked me to help lead the wound-care documentation rollout.

So when I hear the messages through the speakers or the vents, it feels connected to that—like it’s about the records and about me noticing the pattern.\n\nDoctor: During these past two months, have you had a period where you felt unusually energized and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s been happening. I don’t know if I would have called it “energized” at first, because I kept telling myself I was just trying to stay on top of everything, but it’s more than normal tired-but-busy.\n\nI’ll work a full shift, be on my feet all day, and then I get home and instead of crashing like I used to, I’ll start cleaning the kitchen, wiping down counters, reorganizing things, rewriting my notes from work so they’re clearer, sorting supplies or papers into piles. My husband will be sitting there saying, “Please just sit down for a minute,” and I’ll tell him I can’t yet because if I stop, I feel like I’ll lose track of something important.\n\nSometimes I just pace around the kitchen while I’m thinking, back and forth, back and forth, and he gets frustrated because it’s late and he wants me to come to bed. But I don’t feel ready to sleep. My body feels like it still has a motor running, even after the whole day.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you felt like you had special abilities or an unusually important role?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess I have. I don’t mean like, “special abilities” like a superhero or something. It’s not that. It’s more like… I feel like I’m the one who can see what’s actually happening in the wound-care charts when everyone else is brushing it off.\n\nBecause the patterns are there. I know they are. The entries, the times, the way things are coded—it’s not just random mistakes. My supervisor keeps saying, “Oh, those are late entries,” or “That’s just someone fixing documentation,” but I’ve worked with these records enough to know when something doesn’t fit. And it keeps lining up in a way that feels deliberate.\n\nSo yes, I do feel like I have an important role in it, because I caught it. I noticed what management doesn’t want anyone to see. And now when I write things down or flag something, I can tell they’re watching what I put in. It feels like they know I’m the one who saw the pattern, and that makes it hard to just pretend it’s nothing.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you felt unusually happy, euphoric, or especially irritable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not happy exactly, like not carefree happy. It’s more like some days I feel suddenly clear, like I’m finally awake and everybody else is moving too slowly. Like I can see what needs to be fixed at work, and I’m supposed to be the one to fix it or at least point it out.\n\nAnd when I’m in that state, I can get really impatient. If my husband tells me to slow down or sit down, I snap at him. I don’t mean to, but it comes out sharp, like he’s trying to stop me from doing something important. And if a nurse at work tells me I’m overthinking it or says, “Just leave it alone for now,” I get irritated fast, because it feels like they’re blocking me or dismissing something that matters.\n\nSo yes, there are days where I feel… almost charged up, like I have a purpose. But it can turn into anger really quickly if someone gets in my way.\n\nDoctor: Have the voices ever continued during a time when your mood and energy felt back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not when I’m really back to myself.\n\nThe call-light voices and the messages in the charts, that all started after I was already sleeping less and running around like I couldn’t shut off. I was working nonstop, staying up, going over things again and again, feeling like I had to catch every mistake and fix what was happening at the facility. It wasn’t something I’d ever dealt with before, like years ago or during normal weeks.\n\nBefore this whole period where I got so driven and wound up about the rollout, I didn’t hear voices from the call lights or feel like the chart entries had hidden messages for me. It came after I was already… revved up, I guess. Like my mind was locked onto it and I couldn’t let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have there been at least 7 days in a row when you felt this wound-up and needed much less sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely more than seven days. It’s been… I guess about two months now, since they asked me to help lead the wound-care documentation rollout.\n\nAt first I thought it was just because the rollout was stressful, you know, like extra responsibility and everyone needing answers, but it didn’t stop after a few days. It just kept going. Week after week I’d feel wound up, like I had to stay on top of it, and sleep felt like something I could put off because there was too much to do or too much to look over.\n\nSo yes, it hasn’t been just one bad week. My supervisor keeps saying it’s just late entries or coding mistakes, like people are busy and they fix things later, but it doesn’t feel random to me. It’s too consistent.\n\nAnd then when I write something down or flag it, I get this sense that management is watching what I’m putting in the chart, like they know I’m the one who noticed it. I’m not trying to cause trouble, I’m really not, but I caught something they don’t want people to see. So when I hear the messages through the speakers or the vents, it feels connected to that—like it’s about the records and about me noticing the pattern.\n\nDoctor: During these past two months, have you had a period where you felt unusually energized and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s been happening. I don’t know if I would have called it “energized” at first, because I kept telling myself I was just trying to stay on top of everything, but it’s more than normal tired-but-busy.\n\nI’ll work a full shift, be on my feet all day, and then I get home and instead of crashing like I used to, I’ll start cleaning the kitchen, wiping down counters, reorganizing things, rewriting my notes from work so they’re clearer, sorting supplies or papers into piles. My husband will be sitting there saying, “Please just sit down for a minute,” and I’ll tell him I can’t yet because if I stop, I feel like I’ll lose track of something important.\n\nSometimes I just pace around the kitchen while I’m thinking, back and forth, back and forth, and he gets frustrated because it’s late and he wants me to come to bed. But I don’t feel ready to sleep. My body feels like it still has a motor running, even after the whole day.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you felt like you had special abilities or an unusually important role?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess I have. I don’t mean like, “special abilities” like a superhero or something. It’s not that. It’s more like… I feel like I’m the one who can see what’s actually happening in the wound-care charts when everyone else is brushing it off.\n\nBecause the patterns are there. I know they are. The entries, the times, the way things are coded—it’s not just random mistakes. My supervisor keeps saying, “Oh, those are late entries,” or “That’s just someone fixing documentation,” but I’ve worked with these records enough to know when something doesn’t fit. And it keeps lining up in a way that feels deliberate.\n\nSo yes, I do feel like I have an important role in it, because I caught it. I noticed what management doesn’t want anyone to see. And now when I write things down or flag something, I can tell they’re watching what I put in. It feels like they know I’m the one who saw the pattern, and that makes it hard to just pretend it’s nothing.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you felt unusually happy, euphoric, or especially irritable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not happy exactly, like not carefree happy. It’s more like some days I feel suddenly clear, like I’m finally awake and everybody else is moving too slowly. Like I can see what needs to be fixed at work, and I’m supposed to be the one to fix it or at least point it out.\n\nAnd when I’m in that state, I can get really impatient. If my husband tells me to slow down or sit down, I snap at him. I don’t mean to, but it comes out sharp, like he’s trying to stop me from doing something important. And if a nurse at work tells me I’m overthinking it or says, “Just leave it alone for now,” I get irritated fast, because it feels like they’re blocking me or dismissing something that matters.\n\nSo yes, there are days where I feel… almost charged up, like I have a purpose. But it can turn into anger really quickly if someone gets in my way.\n\nDoctor: Have the voices ever continued during a time when your mood and energy felt back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not when I’m really back to myself.\n\nThe call-light voices and the messages in the charts, that all started after I was already sleeping less and running around like I couldn’t shut off. I was working nonstop, staying up, going over things again and again, feeling like I had to catch every mistake and fix what was happening at the facility. It wasn’t something I’d ever dealt with before, like years ago or during normal weeks.\n\nBefore this whole period where I got so driven and wound up about the rollout, I didn’t hear voices from the call lights or feel like the chart entries had hidden messages for me. It came after I was already… revved up, I guess. Like my mind was locked onto it and I couldn’t let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have there been at least 7 days in a row when you felt this wound-up and needed much less sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely more than seven days. It’s been… I guess about two months now, since they asked me to help lead the wound-care documentation rollout.\n\nAt first I thought it was just because the rollout was stressful, you know, like extra responsibility and everyone needing answers, but it didn’t stop after a few days. It just kept going. Week after week I’d feel wound up, like I had to stay on top of it, and sleep felt like something I could put off because there was too much to do or too much to look over.\n\nSo yes, it hasn’t been just one bad week. It’s been going on for around two months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hospitalized because of this episode?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… it’s the voices, mainly. I know how that sounds, but I keep hearing them at work, like coming through the call-light speaker or the hallway vents. It’s not muffled like normal building noise—it’s clear, like somebody is standing right next to me and telling me which patient records have been changed.\n\nAnd it happens even when the unit is quiet. Like when there’s no one out in the hall, no alarms going off, nobody talking at the desk. I’ll hear it, and I’ll look around, and nobody else seems to hear anything. It’s been really upsetting, because it feels so real in the moment, and I can’t just ignore it when it’s about patient records.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been hearing these voices?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two months now, I think. It started around when they asked me to help lead the wound-care documentation rollout at work. At first I thought it was just stress or people talking nearby, because there was so much going on with the records and training people, but it kept happening. So… about two months, since that whole rollout started.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe someone is actually using the speaker or vents to send you these messages?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I think there’s something real happening. I don’t know exactly who is doing it or how, and I know that’s the part that sounds strange, but I can’t just accept that it’s nothing.\n\nThe wound-care charts have a pattern. I’ve been looking at them closely because that’s part of what they had me doing, and there are changes that don’t make sense—entries shifted, things coded a certain way, times that don’t line up. My supervisor keeps saying it’s just late entries or coding mistakes, like people are busy and they fix things later, but it doesn’t feel random to me. It’s too consistent.\n\nAnd then when I write something down or flag it, I get this sense that management is watching what I’m putting in the chart, like they know I’m the one who noticed it. I’m not trying to cause trouble, I’m really not, but I caught something they don’t want people to see. So when I hear the messages through the speakers or the vents, it feels connected to that—like it’s about the records and about me noticing the pattern.\n\nDoctor: During these past two months, have you had a period where you felt unusually energized and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s been happening. I don’t know if I would have called it “energized” at first, because I kept telling myself I was just trying to stay on top of everything, but it’s more than normal tired-but-busy.\n\nI’ll work a full shift, be on my feet all day, and then I get home and instead of crashing like I used to, I’ll start cleaning the kitchen, wiping down counters, reorganizing things, rewriting my notes from work so they’re clearer, sorting supplies or papers into piles. My husband will be sitting there saying, “Please just sit down for a minute,” and I’ll tell him I can’t yet because if I stop, I feel like I’ll lose track of something important.\n\nSometimes I just pace around the kitchen while I’m thinking, back and forth, back and forth, and he gets frustrated because it’s late and he wants me to come to bed. But I don’t feel ready to sleep. My body feels like it still has a motor running, even after the whole day.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you felt like you had special abilities or an unusually important role?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess I have. I don’t mean like, “special abilities” like a superhero or something. It’s not that. It’s more like… I feel like I’m the one who can see what’s actually happening in the wound-care charts when everyone else is brushing it off.\n\nBecause the patterns are there. I know they are. The entries, the times, the way things are coded—it’s not just random mistakes. My supervisor keeps saying, “Oh, those are late entries,” or “That’s just someone fixing documentation,” but I’ve worked with these records enough to know when something doesn’t fit. And it keeps lining up in a way that feels deliberate.\n\nSo yes, I do feel like I have an important role in it, because I caught it. I noticed what management doesn’t want anyone to see. And now when I write things down or flag something, I can tell they’re watching what I put in. It feels like they know I’m the one who saw the pattern, and that makes it hard to just pretend it’s nothing.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you felt unusually happy, euphoric, or especially irritable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not happy exactly, like not carefree happy. It’s more like some days I feel suddenly clear, like I’m finally awake and everybody else is moving too slowly. Like I can see what needs to be fixed at work, and I’m supposed to be the one to fix it or at least point it out.\n\nAnd when I’m in that state, I can get really impatient. If my husband tells me to slow down or sit down, I snap at him. I don’t mean to, but it comes out sharp, like he’s trying to stop me from doing something important. And if a nurse at work tells me I’m overthinking it or says, “Just leave it alone for now,” I get irritated fast, because it feels like they’re blocking me or dismissing something that matters.\n\nSo yes, there are days where I feel… almost charged up, like I have a purpose. But it can turn into anger really quickly if someone gets in my way.\n\nDoctor: Have the voices ever continued during a time when your mood and energy felt back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not when I’m really back to myself.\n\nThe call-light voices and the messages in the charts, that all started after I was already sleeping less and running around like I couldn’t shut off. I was working nonstop, staying up, going over things again and again, feeling like I had to catch every mistake and fix what was happening at the facility. It wasn’t something I’d ever dealt with before, like years ago or during normal weeks.\n\nBefore this whole period where I got so driven and wound up about the rollout, I didn’t hear voices from the call lights or feel like the chart entries had hidden messages for me. It came after I was already… revved up, I guess. Like my mind was locked onto it and I couldn’t let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have there been at least 7 days in a row when you felt this wound-up and needed much less sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely more than seven days. It’s been… I guess about two months now, since they asked me to help lead the wound-care documentation rollout.\n\nAt first I thought it was just because the rollout was stressful, you know, like extra responsibility and everyone needing answers, but it didn’t stop after a few days. It just kept going. Week after week I’d feel wound up, like I had to stay on top of it, and sleep felt like something I could put off because there was too much to do or too much to look over.\n\nSo yes, it hasn’t been just one bad week. It’s been going on for around two months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hospitalized because of this episode?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, I haven’t really thought of it in those terms.\n\nDo you mean admitted, like kept overnight on a unit? Because I don’t think I can say that’s happened from what I know. People have been worried, and there’s been a lot of talk around me about whether I’m okay and whether this has gotten out of hand, but as far as actually being hospitalized… I don’t have a clear yes to give you.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that sounds evasive. I’m not trying to be. I just don’t want to say something wrong.\n\nDoctor: Before this two-month period, have you ever had another separate episode in your life when you felt this same kind of unusually high energy and needed much less sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t really have a clear example of that, no. I’m trying to think back, because I don’t want to just say “never” if I’m forgetting something, but nothing stands out to me like this.\n\nI mean, I’ve had busy stretches in my life, especially with work and responsibilities, where I was tired and pushing through or maybe sleeping less for a few nights because there was a lot going on. But this specific kind of feeling—where it’s like my energy is way up and I don’t seem to need the sleep the way I normally would—I can’t point to another separate time before these last couple months.\n\nSo… as far as I know, no. Not like this.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I can give you a clean yes or no on that. I mean… I’ve had times where I was sad, or worn down, or just felt kind of heavy from life and work and everything, but when you say depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks, I don’t know that I can point to a specific period like that before this.

I’d have to think hard about it, because sometimes when you’re in the middle of things you don’t label it that way, you just keep going, especially in my kind of work. But nothing is coming to mind where I’d say, “Yes, that was definitely two weeks or more of feeling depressed almost every day.”

So… I don’t think so, or at least I’m not aware of one.

Like when there’s no one out in the hall, no alarms going off, nobody talking at the desk. I’ll hear it, and I’ll look around, and nobody else seems to hear anything. It’s been really upsetting, because it feels so real in the moment, and I can’t just ignore it when it’s about patient records.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been hearing these voices?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two months now, I think. It started around when they asked me to help lead the wound-care documentation rollout at work. At first I thought it was just stress or people talking nearby, because there was so much going on with the records and training people, but it kept happening. So… about two months, since that whole rollout started.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe someone is actually using the speaker or vents to send you these messages?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I think there’s something real happening. I don’t know exactly who is doing it or how, and I know that’s the part that sounds strange, but I can’t just accept that it’s nothing.\n\nThe wound-care charts have a pattern. I’ve been looking at them closely because that’s part of what they had me doing, and there are changes that don’t make sense—entries shifted, things coded a certain way, times that don’t line up. My supervisor keeps saying it’s just late entries or coding mistakes, like people are busy and they fix things later, but it doesn’t feel random to me. It’s too consistent.\n\nAnd then when I write something down or flag it, I get this sense that management is watching what I’m putting in the chart, like they know I’m the one who noticed it. I’m not trying to cause trouble, I’m really not, but I caught something they don’t want people to see. So when I hear the messages through the speakers or the vents, it feels connected to that—like it’s about the records and about me noticing the pattern.\n\nDoctor: During these past two months, have you had a period where you felt unusually energized and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s been happening. I don’t know if I would have called it “energized” at first, because I kept telling myself I was just trying to stay on top of everything, but it’s more than normal tired-but-busy.\n\nI’ll work a full shift, be on my feet all day, and then I get home and instead of crashing like I used to, I’ll start cleaning the kitchen, wiping down counters, reorganizing things, rewriting my notes from work so they’re clearer, sorting supplies or papers into piles. My husband will be sitting there saying, “Please just sit down for a minute,” and I’ll tell him I can’t yet because if I stop, I feel like I’ll lose track of something important.\n\nSometimes I just pace around the kitchen while I’m thinking, back and forth, back and forth, and he gets frustrated because it’s late and he wants me to come to bed. But I don’t feel ready to sleep. My body feels like it still has a motor running, even after the whole day.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you felt like you had special abilities or an unusually important role?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess I have. I don’t mean like, “special abilities” like a superhero or something. It’s not that. It’s more like… I feel like I’m the one who can see what’s actually happening in the wound-care charts when everyone else is brushing it off.\n\nBecause the patterns are there. I know they are. The entries, the times, the way things are coded—it’s not just random mistakes. My supervisor keeps saying, “Oh, those are late entries,” or “That’s just someone fixing documentation,” but I’ve worked with these records enough to know when something doesn’t fit. And it keeps lining up in a way that feels deliberate.\n\nSo yes, I do feel like I have an important role in it, because I caught it. I noticed what management doesn’t want anyone to see. And now when I write things down or flag something, I can tell they’re watching what I put in. It feels like they know I’m the one who saw the pattern, and that makes it hard to just pretend it’s nothing.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you felt unusually happy, euphoric, or especially irritable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not happy exactly, like not carefree happy. It’s more like some days I feel suddenly clear, like I’m finally awake and everybody else is moving too slowly. Like I can see what needs to be fixed at work, and I’m supposed to be the one to fix it or at least point it out.\n\nAnd when I’m in that state, I can get really impatient. If my husband tells me to slow down or sit down, I snap at him. I don’t mean to, but it comes out sharp, like he’s trying to stop me from doing something important. And if a nurse at work tells me I’m overthinking it or says, “Just leave it alone for now,” I get irritated fast, because it feels like they’re blocking me or dismissing something that matters.\n\nSo yes, there are days where I feel… almost charged up, like I have a purpose. But it can turn into anger really quickly if someone gets in my way.\n\nDoctor: Have the voices ever continued during a time when your mood and energy felt back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not when I’m really back to myself.\n\nThe call-light voices and the messages in the charts, that all started after I was already sleeping less and running around like I couldn’t shut off. I was working nonstop, staying up, going over things again and again, feeling like I had to catch every mistake and fix what was happening at the facility. It wasn’t something I’d ever dealt with before, like years ago or during normal weeks.\n\nBefore this whole period where I got so driven and wound up about the rollout, I didn’t hear voices from the call lights or feel like the chart entries had hidden messages for me. It came after I was already… revved up, I guess. I mean… I’ve had times where I was sad, or worn down, or just felt kind of heavy from life and work and everything, but when you say depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks, I don’t know that I can point to a specific period like that before this.\n\nI’d have to think hard about it, because sometimes when you’re in the middle of things you don’t label it that way, you just keep going, especially in my kind of work. But nothing is coming to mind where I’d say, “Yes, that was definitely two weeks or more of feeling depressed almost every day.”\n\nSo… I don’t think so, or at least I’m not aware of one.\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore these past two months, had you ever had another period in your life when you heard voices or believed people were sending you hidden messages?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… not before this. Not that I can remember, and I think I would remember something like that because it’s been so unsettling.

The voices with the call lights, that feeling like I was hearing something more than just the normal noise at work, and the sense that there were messages hidden in the charts or the paperwork… that didn’t happen earlier in my life. It started during this whole period where I got so wound up about the rollout, when I was sleeping less and less and just working nonstop. I felt like I had to stay on top of every detail, like if I didn’t fix everything at the facility then something terrible would happen or everything would fall apart.

Before that, I mean, I’ve been stressed at work plenty of times. Call lights go off all day, charts are always a mess, people communicate badly, that’s normal. But I didn’t hear them like voices or think the charts were carrying hidden messages for me. That only started after I got really driven and keyed up about needing to fix everything.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF30.2, F31.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… it’s the voices, mainly. I know how that sounds, but I keep hearing them at work, like coming through the call-light speaker or the hallway vents. It’s not muffled like normal building noise—it’s clear, like somebody is standing right next to me and telling me which patient records have been changed.\n\nAnd it happens even when the unit is quiet. Like when there’s no one out in the hall, no alarms going off, nobody talking at the desk. I’ll hear it, and I’ll look around, and nobody else seems to hear anything. It’s been really upsetting, because it feels so real in the moment, and I can’t just ignore it when it’s about patient records.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been hearing these voices?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two months now, I think. It started around when they asked me to help lead the wound-care documentation rollout at work. At first I thought it was just stress or people talking nearby, because there was so much going on with the records and training people, but it kept happening. So… about two months, since that whole rollout started.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe someone is actually using the speaker or vents to send you these messages?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I think there’s something real happening. I don’t know exactly who is doing it or how, and I know that’s the part that sounds strange, but I can’t just accept that it’s nothing.\n\nThe wound-care charts have a pattern. I’ve been looking at them closely because that’s part of what they had me doing, and there are changes that don’t make sense—entries shifted, things coded a certain way, times that don’t line up. My supervisor keeps saying it’s just late entries or coding mistakes, like people are busy and they fix things later, but it doesn’t feel random to me. It’s too consistent.\n\nAnd then when I write something down or flag it, I get this sense that management is watching what I’m putting in the chart, like they know I’m the one who noticed it. I’m not trying to cause trouble, I’m really not, but I caught something they don’t want people to see. So when I hear the messages through the speakers or the vents, it feels connected to that—like it’s about the records and about me noticing the pattern.\n\nDoctor: During these past two months, have you had a period where you felt unusually energized and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s been happening. I don’t know if I would have called it “energized” at first, because I kept telling myself I was just trying to stay on top of everything, but it’s more than normal tired-but-busy.\n\nI’ll work a full shift, be on my feet all day, and then I get home and instead of crashing like I used to, I’ll start cleaning the kitchen, wiping down counters, reorganizing things, rewriting my notes from work so they’re clearer, sorting supplies or papers into piles. My husband will be sitting there saying, “Please just sit down for a minute,” and I’ll tell him I can’t yet because if I stop, I feel like I’ll lose track of something important.\n\nSometimes I just pace around the kitchen while I’m thinking, back and forth, back and forth, and he gets frustrated because it’s late and he wants me to come to bed. But I don’t feel ready to sleep. My body feels like it still has a motor running, even after the whole day.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you felt like you had special abilities or an unusually important role?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess I have. I don’t mean like, “special abilities” like a superhero or something. It’s not that. It’s more like… I feel like I’m the one who can see what’s actually happening in the wound-care charts when everyone else is brushing it off.\n\nBecause the patterns are there. I know they are. The entries, the times, the way things are coded—it’s not just random mistakes. My supervisor keeps saying, “Oh, those are late entries,” or “That’s just someone fixing documentation,” but I’ve worked with these records enough to know when something doesn’t fit. And it keeps lining up in a way that feels deliberate.\n\nSo yes, I do feel like I have an important role in it, because I caught it. I noticed what management doesn’t want anyone to see. And now when I write things down or flag something, I can tell they’re watching what I put in. It feels like they know I’m the one who saw the pattern, and that makes it hard to just pretend it’s nothing.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you felt unusually happy, euphoric, or especially irritable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not happy exactly, like not carefree happy. It’s more like some days I feel suddenly clear, like I’m finally awake and everybody else is moving too slowly. Like I can see what needs to be fixed at work, and I’m supposed to be the one to fix it or at least point it out.\n\nAnd when I’m in that state, I can get really impatient. If my husband tells me to slow down or sit down, I snap at him. I don’t mean to, but it comes out sharp, like he’s trying to stop me from doing something important. And if a nurse at work tells me I’m overthinking it or says, “Just leave it alone for now,” I get irritated fast, because it feels like they’re blocking me or dismissing something that matters.\n\nSo yes, there are days where I feel… almost charged up, like I have a purpose. But it can turn into anger really quickly if someone gets in my way.\n\nDoctor: Have the voices ever continued during a time when your mood and energy felt back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not when I’m really back to myself.\n\nThe call-light voices and the messages in the charts, that all started after I was already sleeping less and running around like I couldn’t shut off. I was working nonstop, staying up, going over things again and again, feeling like I had to catch every mistake and fix what was happening at the facility. It wasn’t something I’d ever dealt with before, like years ago or during normal weeks.\n\nBefore this whole period where I got so driven and wound up about the rollout, I didn’t hear voices from the call lights or feel like the chart entries had hidden messages for me. It came after I was already… revved up, I guess. Like my mind was locked onto it and I couldn’t let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have there been at least 7 days in a row when you felt this wound-up and needed much less sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely more than seven days. It’s been… I guess about two months now, since they asked me to help lead the wound-care documentation rollout.\n\nAt first I thought it was just because the rollout was stressful, you know, like extra responsibility and everyone needing answers, but it didn’t stop after a few days. It just kept going. Week after week I’d feel wound up, like I had to stay on top of it, and sleep felt like something I could put off because there was too much to do or too much to look over.\n\nSo yes, it hasn’t been just one bad week. It’s been going on for around two months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hospitalized because of this episode?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, I haven’t really thought of it in those terms.\n\nDo you mean admitted, like kept overnight on a unit? Because I don’t think I can say that’s happened from what I know. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Before these past two months? No, not really. I mean, I’ve had bad moods before, like anybody, and I’ve gotten irritated when work was busy or people weren’t doing what they were supposed to do. But not like this.

This has been different. Some days it’s almost like I’m finally awake, like I can see what needs to be fixed at work and everybody else is moving too slowly or missing the point. I’ll feel like I have all this energy and purpose, like I’m supposed to straighten everything out, and it feels important, not just like regular motivation.

And then if my husband tells me I need to slow down, or if a nurse at work acts like I’m doing too much or should back off, I get really sharp with them. I snap because in that moment it feels like they’re getting in the way of something that matters. Later I can think, okay, maybe I came on too strong, but at the time it feels like they’re blocking me.

So before this, I don’t think I had a period like that. Not with that kind of “I’m awake and I have to fix everything” feeling, and not with the irritability being so intense.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… it’s the voices, mainly. I know how that sounds, but I keep hearing them at work, like coming through the call-light speaker or the hallway vents. It’s not muffled like normal building noise—it’s clear, like somebody is standing right next to me and telling me which patient records have been changed.\n\nAnd it happens even when the unit is quiet. Like when there’s no one out in the hall, no alarms going off, nobody talking at the desk. I’ll hear it, and I’ll look around, and nobody else seems to hear anything. It’s been really upsetting, because it feels so real in the moment, and I can’t just ignore it when it’s about patient records.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been hearing these voices?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two months now, I think. It started around when they asked me to help lead the wound-care documentation rollout at work. At first I thought it was just stress or people talking nearby, because there was so much going on with the records and training people, but it kept happening. So… about two months, since that whole rollout started.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe someone is actually using the speaker or vents to send you these messages?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I think there’s something real happening. I don’t know exactly who is doing it or how, and I know that’s the part that sounds strange, but I can’t just accept that it’s nothing.\n\nThe wound-care charts have a pattern. I’ve been looking at them closely because that’s part of what they had me doing, and there are changes that don’t make sense—entries shifted, things coded a certain way, times that don’t line up. My supervisor keeps saying it’s just late entries or coding mistakes, like people are busy and they fix things later, but it doesn’t feel random to me. It’s too consistent.\n\nAnd then when I write something down or flag it, I get this sense that management is watching what I’m putting in the chart, like they know I’m the one who noticed it. I’m not trying to cause trouble, I’m really not, but I caught something they don’t want people to see. So when I hear the messages through the speakers or the vents, it feels connected to that—like it’s about the records and about me noticing the pattern.\n\nDoctor: During these past two months, have you had a period where you felt unusually energized and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s been happening. I don’t know if I would have called it “energized” at first, because I kept telling myself I was just trying to stay on top of everything, but it’s more than normal tired-but-busy.\n\nI’ll work a full shift, be on my feet all day, and then I get home and instead of crashing like I used to, I’ll start cleaning the kitchen, wiping down counters, reorganizing things, rewriting my notes from work so they’re clearer, sorting supplies or papers into piles. My husband will be sitting there saying, “Please just sit down for a minute,” and I’ll tell him I can’t yet because if I stop, I feel like I’ll lose track of something important.\n\nSometimes I just pace around the kitchen while I’m thinking, back and forth, back and forth, and he gets frustrated because it’s late and he wants me to come to bed. But I don’t feel ready to sleep. My body feels like it still has a motor running, even after the whole day.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you felt like you had special abilities or an unusually important role?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess I have. I don’t mean like, “special abilities” like a superhero or something. It’s not that. It’s more like… I feel like I’m the one who can see what’s actually happening in the wound-care charts when everyone else is brushing it off.\n\nBecause the patterns are there. I know they are. The entries, the times, the way things are coded—it’s not just random mistakes. My supervisor keeps saying, “Oh, those are late entries,” or “That’s just someone fixing documentation,” but I’ve worked with these records enough to know when something doesn’t fit. And it keeps lining up in a way that feels deliberate.\n\nSo yes, I do feel like I have an important role in it, because I caught it. I noticed what management doesn’t want anyone to see. And now when I write things down or flag something, I can tell they’re watching what I put in. It feels like they know I’m the one who saw the pattern, and that makes it hard to just pretend it’s nothing.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you felt unusually happy, euphoric, or especially irritable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not happy exactly, like not carefree happy. It’s more like some days I feel suddenly clear, like I’m finally awake and everybody else is moving too slowly. Like I can see what needs to be fixed at work, and I’m supposed to be the one to fix it or at least point it out.\n\nAnd when I’m in that state, I can get really impatient. If my husband tells me to slow down or sit down, I snap at him. I don’t mean to, but it comes out sharp, like he’s trying to stop me from doing something important. And if a nurse at work tells me I’m overthinking it or says, “Just leave it alone for now,” I get irritated fast, because it feels like they’re blocking me or dismissing something that matters.\n\nSo yes, there are days where I feel… almost charged up, like I have a purpose. But it can turn into anger really quickly if someone gets in my way.\n\nDoctor: Have the voices ever continued during a time when your mood and energy felt back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not when I’m really back to myself.\n\nThe call-light voices and the messages in the charts, that all started after I was already sleeping less and running around like I couldn’t shut off. I was working nonstop, staying up, going over things again and again, feeling like I had to catch every mistake and fix what was happening at the facility. It wasn’t something I’d ever dealt with before, like years ago or during normal weeks.\n\nBefore this whole period where I got so driven and wound up about the rollout, I didn’t hear voices from the call lights or feel like the chart entries had hidden messages for me. It came after I was already… revved up, I guess. Like my mind was locked onto it and I couldn’t let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have there been at least 7 days in a row when you felt this wound-up and needed much less sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely more than seven days. It’s been… I guess about two months now, since they asked me to help lead the wound-care documentation rollout.\n\nAt first I thought it was just because the rollout was stressful, you know, like extra responsibility and everyone needing answers, but it didn’t stop after a few days. It just kept going. Week after week I’d feel wound up, like I had to stay on top of it, and sleep felt like something I could put off because there was too much to do or too much to look over.\n\nSo yes, it hasn’t been just one bad week. It’s been going on for around two months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hospitalized because of this episode?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, I haven’t really thought of it in those terms.\n\nDo you mean admitted, like kept overnight on a unit? Because I don’t think I can say that’s happened from what I know. People have been worried, and there’s been a lot of talk around me about whether I’m okay and whether this has gotten out of hand, but as far as actually being hospitalized… I don’t have a clear yes to give you.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that sounds evasive. I’m not trying to be. I just don’t want to say something wrong.\n\nDoctor: Before this two-month period, have you ever had another separate episode in your life when you felt this same kind of unusually high energy and needed much less sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t really have a clear example of that, no. I’m trying to think back, because I don’t want to just say “never” if I’m forgetting something, but nothing stands out to me like this.\n\nI mean, I’ve had busy stretches in my life, especially with work and responsibilities, where I was tired and pushing through or maybe sleeping less for a few nights because there was a lot going on. But this specific kind of feeling—where it’s like my energy is way up and I don’t seem to need the sleep the way I normally would—I can’t point to another separate time before these last couple months.\n\nSo… as far as I know, no. Not like this.\n\nDoctor: Before this two-month period, have you ever had a time in your life when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean yes or no on that. I mean… I’ve had times where I was sad, or worn down, or just felt kind of heavy from life and work and everything, but when you say depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks, I don’t know that I can point to a specific period like that before this.\n\nI’d have to think hard about it, because sometimes when you’re in the middle of things you don’t label it that way, you just keep going, especially in my kind of work. Not that I can remember, and I think I would remember something like that because it’s been so unsettling.\n\nThe voices with the call lights, that feeling like I was hearing something more than just the normal noise at work, and the sense that there were messages hidden in the charts or the paperwork… that didn’t happen earlier in my life. I don’t think so. I mean, before these last two months, I still did my usual things when I had the chance. I wouldn’t say I was always thrilled or relaxed — life is busy, and work takes a lot out of you — but I don’t remember a stretch where I just lost interest in almost everything.

I’ve been looking at them closely because that’s part of what they had me doing, and there are changes that don’t make sense—entries shifted, things coded a certain way, times that don’t line up. My supervisor keeps saying it’s just late entries or coding mistakes, like people are busy and they fix things later, but it doesn’t feel random to me. It’s too consistent.\n\nAnd then when I write something down or flag it, I get this sense that management is watching what I’m putting in the chart, like they know I’m the one who noticed it. I’m not trying to cause trouble, I’m really not, but I caught something they don’t want people to see. So when I hear the messages through the speakers or the vents, it feels connected to that—like it’s about the records and about me noticing the pattern.\n\nDoctor: During these past two months, have you had a period where you felt unusually energized and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s been happening. I don’t know if I would have called it “energized” at first, because I kept telling myself I was just trying to stay on top of everything, but it’s more than normal tired-but-busy.\n\nI’ll work a full shift, be on my feet all day, and then I get home and instead of crashing like I used to, I’ll start cleaning the kitchen, wiping down counters, reorganizing things, rewriting my notes from work so they’re clearer, sorting supplies or papers into piles. My husband will be sitting there saying, “Please just sit down for a minute,” and I’ll tell him I can’t yet because if I stop, I feel like I’ll lose track of something important.\n\nSometimes I just pace around the kitchen while I’m thinking, back and forth, back and forth, and he gets frustrated because it’s late and he wants me to come to bed. But I don’t feel ready to sleep. My body feels like it still has a motor running, even after the whole day.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you felt like you had special abilities or an unusually important role?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess I have. I don’t mean like, “special abilities” like a superhero or something. It’s not that. It’s more like… I feel like I’m the one who can see what’s actually happening in the wound-care charts when everyone else is brushing it off.\n\nBecause the patterns are there. I know they are. The entries, the times, the way things are coded—it’s not just random mistakes. My supervisor keeps saying, “Oh, those are late entries,” or “That’s just someone fixing documentation,” but I’ve worked with these records enough to know when something doesn’t fit. And it keeps lining up in a way that feels deliberate.\n\nSo yes, I do feel like I have an important role in it, because I caught it. I noticed what management doesn’t want anyone to see. And now when I write things down or flag something, I can tell they’re watching what I put in. It feels like they know I’m the one who saw the pattern, and that makes it hard to just pretend it’s nothing.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you felt unusually happy, euphoric, or especially irritable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not happy exactly, like not carefree happy. It’s more like some days I feel suddenly clear, like I’m finally awake and everybody else is moving too slowly. Like I can see what needs to be fixed at work, and I’m supposed to be the one to fix it or at least point it out.\n\nAnd when I’m in that state, I can get really impatient. If my husband tells me to slow down or sit down, I snap at him. I don’t mean to, but it comes out sharp, like he’s trying to stop me from doing something important. And if a nurse at work tells me I’m overthinking it or says, “Just leave it alone for now,” I get irritated fast, because it feels like they’re blocking me or dismissing something that matters.\n\nSo yes, there are days where I feel… almost charged up, like I have a purpose. But it can turn into anger really quickly if someone gets in my way.\n\nDoctor: Have the voices ever continued during a time when your mood and energy felt back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not when I’m really back to myself.\n\nThe call-light voices and the messages in the charts, that all started after I was already sleeping less and running around like I couldn’t shut off. I was working nonstop, staying up, going over things again and again, feeling like I had to catch every mistake and fix what was happening at the facility. It wasn’t something I’d ever dealt with before, like years ago or during normal weeks.\n\nBefore this whole period where I got so driven and wound up about the rollout, I didn’t hear voices from the call lights or feel like the chart entries had hidden messages for me. It came after I was already… revved up, I guess. Like my mind was locked onto it and I couldn’t let it go.\n\nDoctor: Have there been at least 7 days in a row when you felt this wound-up and needed much less sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely more than seven days. It’s been… I guess about two months now, since they asked me to help lead the wound-care documentation rollout.\n\nAt first I thought it was just because the rollout was stressful, you know, like extra responsibility and everyone needing answers, but it didn’t stop after a few days. It just kept going. Week after week I’d feel wound up, like I had to stay on top of it, and sleep felt like something I could put off because there was too much to do or too much to look over.\n\nSo yes, it hasn’t been just one bad week. It’s been going on for around two months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hospitalized because of this episode?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, I haven’t really thought of it in those terms.\n\nDo you mean admitted, like kept overnight on a unit? Because I don’t think I can say that’s happened from what I know. People have been worried, and there’s been a lot of talk around me about whether I’m okay and whether this has gotten out of hand, but as far as actually being hospitalized… I don’t have a clear yes to give you.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that sounds evasive. I’m not trying to be. I just don’t want to say something wrong.\n\nDoctor: Before this two-month period, have you ever had another separate episode in your life when you felt this same kind of unusually high energy and needed much less sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t really have a clear example of that, no. I’m trying to think back, because I don’t want to just say “never” if I’m forgetting something, but nothing stands out to me like this.\n\nI mean, I’ve had busy stretches in my life, especially with work and responsibilities, where I was tired and pushing through or maybe sleeping less for a few nights because there was a lot going on. But this specific kind of feeling—where it’s like my energy is way up and I don’t seem to need the sleep the way I normally would—I can’t point to another separate time before these last couple months.\n\nSo… as far as I know, no. Not like this.\n\nDoctor: Before this two-month period, have you ever had a time in your life when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean yes or no on that. I mean… I’ve had times where I was sad, or worn down, or just felt kind of heavy from life and work and everything, but when you say depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks, I don’t know that I can point to a specific period like that before this.\n\nI’d have to think hard about it, because sometimes when you’re in the middle of things you don’t label it that way, you just keep going, especially in my kind of work. But nothing is coming to mind where I’d say, “Yes, that was definitely two weeks or more of feeling depressed almost every day.”\n\nSo… I don’t think so, or at least I’m not aware of one.\n\nDoctor: Before these past two months, had you ever had another period in your life when you heard voices or believed people were sending you hidden messages?\n\nPatient: No… not before this. Not that I can remember, and I think I would remember something like that because it’s been so unsettling.\n\nThe voices with the call lights, that feeling like I was hearing something more than just the normal noise at work, and the sense that there were messages hidden in the charts or the paperwork… that didn’t happen earlier in my life. It started during this whole period where I got so wound up about the rollout, when I was sleeping less and less and just working nonstop. I felt like I had to stay on top of every detail, like if I didn’t fix everything at the facility then something terrible would happen or everything would fall apart.\n\nBefore that, I mean, I’ve been stressed at work plenty of times. Call lights go off all day, charts are always a mess, people communicate badly, that’s normal. But I didn’t hear them like voices or think the charts were carrying hidden messages for me. That only started after I got really driven and keyed up about needing to fix everything.\n\nDoctor: At any time before these past two months, did you ever have a period when you felt unusually high or irritable and had far more energy than usual?\n\nPatient: Before these past two months? No, not really. I mean, I’ve had bad moods before, like anybody, and I’ve gotten irritated when work was busy or people weren’t doing what they were supposed to do. But not like this.\n\nThis has been different. Some days it’s almost like I’m finally awake, like I can see what needs to be fixed at work and everybody else is moving too slowly or missing the point. I’ll feel like I have all this energy and purpose, like I’m supposed to straighten everything out, and it feels important, not just like regular motivation.\n\nAnd then if my husband tells me I need to slow down, or if a nurse at work acts like I’m doing too much or should back off, I get really sharp with them. I snap because in that moment it feels like they’re getting in the way of something that matters. Later I can think, okay, maybe I came on too strong, but at the time it feels like they’re blocking me.\n\nSo before this, I don’t think I had a period like that. Not with that kind of “I’m awake and I have to fix everything” feeling, and not with the irritability being so intense.\n\nDoctor: Before these past two months, did you ever have a time when you lost interest in almost everything you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, before these last two months, I still did my usual things when I had the chance. I wouldn’t say I was always thrilled or relaxed — life is busy, and work takes a lot out of you — but I don’t remember a stretch where I just lost interest in almost everything.\n\nThere were times I was tired, sure, or I didn’t feel like doing much after a long shift, but that felt more like being worn out than not caring anymore. I still cared about my normal routines, my family, the things I usually pay attention to. So… no, I don’t think I had that before this.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I’ll feel like I have all this energy and purpose, like I’m supposed to straighten everything out, and it feels important, not just like regular motivation.\n\nAnd then if my husband tells me I need to slow down, or if a nurse at work acts like I’m doing too much or should back off, I get really sharp with them. I snap because in that moment it feels like they’re getting in the way of something that matters. Later I can think, okay, maybe I came on too strong, but at the time it feels like they’re blocking me.\n\nSo before this, I don’t think I had a period like that. Not with that kind of “I’m awake and I have to fix everything” feeling, and not with the irritability being so intense.\n\nDoctor: Before these past two months, did you ever have a time when you lost interest in almost everything you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, before these last two months, I still did my usual things when I had the chance. I wouldn’t say I was always thrilled or relaxed — life is busy, and work takes a lot out of you — but I don’t remember a stretch where I just lost interest in almost everything.\n\nThere were times I was tired, sure, or I didn’t feel like doing much after a long shift, but that felt more like being worn out than not caring anymore. I still cared about my normal routines, my family, the things I usually pay attention to. So… no, I don’t think I had that before this.\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore these last two months, was there ever a time when you felt hopeless nearly every day?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 15\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }
]

{"key":"None","name":"None","description":"None","reason":"The doctor is asking about a prior depressive symptom, specifically hopelessness nearly every day before the last two months. Hopeless nearly every day… before these last two months? I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had hard stretches, of course. There were days where work felt endless or I’d come home drained and think, “I can’t keep doing this pace forever,” but that’s different from feeling hopeless every day.

I wouldn’t say I was walking around feeling like there was no point or no future. I’d get frustrated, overwhelmed, tired, maybe discouraged for a day or two, but I usually still felt like things could be handled somehow. So… no, not that I can remember. Not nearly every day like that.
